no result bounds, plus one new Boolean method
{\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger}
on each new relation

R_{\mathsf{mt}}
, that has no result bounds either.

{exa}

Recall the schema
{\mathsf{Sch}}
of Examples
1
and
1
.
It featured a relation

{\mathsf{Prof}}(\mathit{id},\mathit{name},\mathit{salary})

with an access method
\mathsf{pr}
having input
\mathit{id}
to obtain
information about a professor; and featured a relation
{\mathsf{Udirectory}}(\mathit{id},\mathit{address},\allowbreak\mathit{phone})
with
an access method

\mathsf{ud}_{2}
taking an
\mathit{id}
as input and returning the

\mathit{address}
and phone number of tuples with this
\mathit{id}
.
We assumed a result bound of
1
on
\mathsf{ud}_{2}
, and assumed
the functional dependency
\phi
: each employee id
has exactly one
\mathit{address}
(but possibly
many phone numbers).

The existence-check simplification of
{\mathsf{Sch}}

has a signature with relations
{\mathsf{Udirectory}}
,
{\mathsf{Prof}}
, and a new relation

{\mathsf{Udirectory}}_{\mathsf{ud}_{2}}
of arity
1
.
It has two access methods without result bounds: the method
\mathsf{pr}

on
{\mathsf{Prof}}
like in
{\mathsf{Sch}}
, and a Boolean method
\mathsf{ud}_{2}^{\prime}
on

{\mathsf{Udirectory}}_{\mathsf{ud}_{2}}
. Its constraints are those of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
, plus the
following IDs:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Udirectory}}(i,a,p)\rightarrow

\displaystyle~{\mathsf{Udirectory}}_{\mathsf{ud}_{2}}(i)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Udirectory}}_{\mathsf{ud}_{2}}(i)\rightarrow

\displaystyle~\exists a\,p~{\mathsf{Udirectory}}(i,a,p)

Clearly, every plan that uses the existence-check simplification

{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
of a schema

{\mathsf{Sch}}
can be converted into a plan using
{\mathsf{Sch}}
, by replacing the
accesses on the Boolean method of
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
to non-deterministic accesses
with
{\mathsf{mt}}
, and only checking whether the result of these accesses is empty.
We want to understand when the converse is true. That is, when a plan on
{\mathsf{Sch}}
can
be converted to a plan on
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
. For instance,
recalling the plan of Example
1
that tests
whether
{\mathsf{Udirectory}}
is empty simply by accessing
\mathsf{ud}_{2}
,
we could implement it in the existence-check
simplification of this schema.
More generally, we want to identify schemas
{\mathsf{Sch}}
for which
any

CQ having a monotone plan over
{\mathsf{Sch}}
has a plan
on the existence-check
simplification
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
.
We say that

{\mathsf{Sch}}

is
existence-check simplifiable
when this holds:
this intuitively means that
“result-bounded methods of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
are only useful for existence checks”.

Showing existence-check simplifiability.

We first show that this notion of existence-check simplifiability holds for schemas like Example
1
whose constraints consist of
inclusion dependencies:

Theorem 10
.

Let
{\mathsf{Sch}}
be a schema whose constraints are

ID
s, and let
Q
be a CQ that is
monotonically answerable
in
{\mathsf{Sch}}
.
Then
Q
is monotonically answerable in the existence-check simplification
of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
.

This existence-check simplifiability result implies in particular that

for schemas with
ID
s, monotone answerability is decidable even
with result bounds
. This is because the existence-check simplification of the
schema features only
ID
s and no result bounds,
so the query containment problem for
{\mathsf{AMonDet}}

only features guarded TGDs, which implies decidability. We will show a finer
complexity bound in the next section.

To prove Theorem
10
, we show that if
Q
is not
{\mathsf{AMonDet}}

in the existence-check simplification
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
, then it cannot be
{\mathsf{AMonDet}}

in
{\mathsf{Sch}}
. This
suffices to prove the contrapositive of the result, because

{\mathsf{AMonDet}}
is equivalent
to monotone answerability (Theorem
4
).
As in all of our results concerning entailment problems like
{\mathsf{AMonDet}}
, in the proof
for simplicity we will assume the query
Q
is Boolean. The general
case is handled by simply considering free variables as
additional constants.

Let us show, for a Boolean query
Q
, that
Q
not being
{\mathsf{AMonDet}}
in

{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
implies
that it is not
{\mathsf{AMonDet}}
in
{\mathsf{Sch}}
. To do so,
we introduce a general method of

blowing up models
that we will reuse in all subsequent simplifiability
results. We assume that
{\mathsf{AMonDet}}
does not hold in the
simplification
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
, and consider
a
counterexample to
{\mathsf{AMonDet}}
for
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
:
two instances
I_{1}^{\dagger},I_{2}^{\dagger}
both satisfying the schema constraints,
such that
I_{1}^{\dagger}
satisfies
Q
while
I_{2}^{\dagger}
satisfies
\neg Q
, and

I_{1}^{\dagger}
and
I_{2}^{\dagger}
have a
common subinstance
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
which is access-valid in
I_{1}^{\dagger}
.
We use them to build a counterexample to
{\mathsf{AMonDet}}

for the original schema
{\mathsf{Sch}}
: we will always do so by adding more facts
to
I_{1}^{\dagger}
and
I_{2}^{\dagger}

and then restricting to the relations of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
.
We formalize the sufficiency of such a construction in the following lemma,
whose proof is immediate, and which we state in full generality as we will use
it in multiple places:

Lemma 11
.

Let
{\mathsf{Sch}}
and
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
be
schemas and
Q
a CQ on the common relations of
{\mathsf{Sch}}

and
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}

such that
Q
is not
{\mathsf{AMonDet}}
in
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
.
Suppose that for some counterexample
I_{1}^{\dagger},I_{2}^{\dagger}
to
{\mathsf{AMonDet}}
for
Q

in
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}

we can construct instances
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
over
{\mathsf{Sch}}
, satisfying the constraints of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
,
which have a common subinstance

I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
that is access-valid in
I_{1}
for
{\mathsf{Sch}}
, such that
I_{2}
has
a homomorphism to
I_{2}^{\dagger}
, and such that
the restriction of
I_{1}^{\dagger}
to the relations of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
is a subinstance
of
I_{1}
.
Then
Q
is not
{\mathsf{AMonDet}}
in
{\mathsf{Sch}}
.

Proof 5.1
.

The instances
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
satisfy the constraints of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
and they have
a common subinstance which is access-valid in
I_{1}
for
{\mathsf{Sch}}
.
Recall that, by definition of a counterexample,
I_{1}^{\dagger}
satisfies
Q

and
I_{2}^{\dagger}
does not.
Now, instance
I_{1}
satisfies
Q
, because
I_{1}^{\dagger}
does and
Q
only
mentions the relations of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
, and
I_{2}
does not
satisfy
Q
, because it has a homomorphism to
I_{2}^{\dagger}
which does not.
Hence,
I_{1},I_{2}
is a counterexample showing that
Q
is not
{\mathsf{AMonDet}}

in
{\mathsf{Sch}}
.

Using this lemma, we can now prove Theorem
10
:

Proof 5.2
.

We use the equivalence
between
{\mathsf{AMonDet}}
and monotone plans given by Theorem
4
, and we
prove the contrapositive of the theorem, using
Lemma
11
.
Let
{\mathsf{Sch}}
be the original
schema and
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
be the existence-check simplification.
Notice that the query
Q
is indeed posed on the common relations of
{\mathsf{Sch}}

and
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
, i.e., it does not involve the
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
relations added
in
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
.
To use Lemma
11
, suppose that we have a counterexample

(I_{1}^{\dagger},I_{2}^{\dagger})
to

{\mathsf{AMonDet}}
for
Q
and the simplification
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
, i.e.,
the instances
I_{1}^{\dagger}
and
I_{2}^{\dagger}
satisfy the constraints
\Sigma^{\dagger}

of
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
, the instance
I_{1}^{\dagger}
satisfies
Q
and
the instance
I_{2}^{\dagger}
violates
Q
, and
I_{1}^{\dagger}
and
I_{2}^{\dagger}

have a common subinstance
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
that is
access-valid in
I_{1}^{\dagger}
.
We will show how to “blow up” each instance
to
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
which have a common subinstance which is
access-valid in
I_{1}
,
i.e., we must ensure that each access to a method with
a result bound in
I_{1}
returns either no tuples or more tuples than the bound.
In the blowup process we will
preserve the constraints
\Sigma^{\dagger}
and the properties of the
I_{i}
with respect
to the CQ
Q
. Intuitively, the blowup process will consider all accesses that
can be performed with the common subinstance
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
, and instantiate
infinitely many witnesses to serve as answers for these accesses. We will then
repair the instances by applying chase steps so that they satisfy the constraints again.

We now explain formally how
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
are formed.
The first step is “obliviously chasing with the existence-check constraints”:
for any existence-check constraint
\delta

of the form

\forall x_{1}\ldots x_{m}~R_{\mathsf{mt}}(\vec{x})\rightarrow~\exists y_{1}\ldots y_{n}~R(\vec{x},\vec{y})

and any homomorphism
h
of the variables
x_{1}\ldots x_{m}
to
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
,
we extend the mapping by choosing infinitely many fresh witnesses for
y_{1}\ldots y_{n}
, naming the
j^{th}
value for
y_{i}

in some canonical way
depending on
(h(x_{1}),\ldots h(x_{m}),\delta,j,i)
, and creating the
corresponding facts.
We use the term “obliviously chasing” to emphasize that the trigger
may not be active.
We let
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{*}
be
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
extended with these facts.

The second step is “chasing with the original constraints”.
Recall the definition of “the chase” in Section
3
.
Specifically, we let
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
be the chase of
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{*}

by
\Sigma
.

We now construct
I_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}I_{1}^{\dagger}\cup I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}

and similarly define
I_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}I_{2}^{\dagger}\cup I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
.
We also remove all facts from
I_{1}
,
I_{2}
, and
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
where the underlying relation
is not in
{\mathsf{Sch}}
.

We now show correctness. First observe that
the restriction of
I_{1}^{\dagger}
to the relations of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
is a subinstance of

I_{1}
, so that
I_{1}
still satisfies
Q
.
Further,
we argue that for all
p\in\{1,2\}
, the instance
I_{p}
satisfies
\Sigma
.
As
\Sigma
consists only of
ID
s,
its triggers consist of single facts, so it suffices to check this
on
I_{p}^{\dagger}
and on
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
separately. For
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
, we know that it satisfies
\Sigma
by
definition of the chase. For
I_{p}^{\dagger}
, we know it satisfied
\Sigma^{\dagger}
(before the
last step of removing the facts not on relations of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
), so it
satisfies
\Sigma
.

We must now justify that
I_{2}

has a homomorphism
h
to
I_{2}^{\dagger}
, which will imply that it still does not satisfy
Q
.
We
first define
h
to be the identity on
I_{2}^{\dagger}
.
It then suffices to define
h
as a homomorphism from
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}

to
I_{2}^{\dagger}

which is the identity on
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
, because
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}\cap I_{2}^{\dagger}=I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
.
We next define
h
on
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{*}\setminus I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
. Consider a fact

F=R(\vec{a})
of
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{*}\setminus I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
created by
obliviously chasing
a trigger on an existence-check constraint
\delta
on
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
.
Let
F^{\prime}=S(\vec{b})
be the
fact of
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
in the image of the trigger: that is, the fact that matches the body of

\delta
.
We know that
\delta
holds in
I_{2}^{\dagger}
and thus there is some
fact
F^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}R(\vec{c})
in
I_{2}^{\dagger}

that serves as a witness for this. Writing
{\mathsf{Arity}}(R)
to denote the
arity of
R
,
we define
h(a_{i})

for each
1\leq i\leq{\mathsf{Arity}}(R)
as
h(a_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}c_{i}
. In this way, the image
of the fact
F
under
h
is
F^{\prime\prime}
.

We argue that this is consistent with the stipulation that
h
is the identity
on
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
. This is
because whenever
a_{i}\in{\mathsf{Adom}}(I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger})
,
a_{i}
was
not a fresh element when firing the trigger that created
F
. So
c_{i}
was not
fresh either and must have been the same element, i.e.,
c_{i}=a_{i}
.

Further, we claim that all these assignments are consistent across the facts of

I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{*}\setminus I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
because all elements of
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{*}\setminus I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
which do not occur in
{\mathsf{Adom}}(I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger})
occur at exactly one position in
one fact of
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{*}\setminus I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
.

We now define
h
on facts of
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}\setminus I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{*}
inductively by extending it
on the new elements introduced throughout the chase.
Whenever we create a fact
F=R(\vec{a})
in
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
for
a trigger
\tau
mapping to
F^{\prime}=S(\vec{b})
for an
ID

\delta
in
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
, we explain how to
extend
h
to the nulls introduced in
F
. Consider the fact
h(F^{\prime})=S(h(\vec{b}))
in
I_{2}^{\dagger}
. The body of
\delta
also matches this fact, and
as
I_{2}^{\dagger}
satisfies
\Sigma^{\dagger}

there must be a fact
F^{\prime\prime}=R(\vec{c})
in
I_{2}^{\dagger}
which extends this match to the
head of
\delta
,
since
\delta
holds in
I_{2}^{\dagger}
.
For the elements
a_{i}
that are not nulls created when firing
\tau
, the image

h(a_{i})
of
a_{i}
by
h
is already defined, and more precisely we must have

h(a_{i})=c_{i}
, by the same reasoning as when we defined
h
on
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{*}\setminus I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
. Now, for the
a_{i}
’s that are nulls, noting that all
of them are distinct, we simply set
h(a_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}c_{i}
. This ensures
that
h(F)=F^{\prime\prime}
, so
F
has a homomorphic image. Hence, performing this
process inductively indeed creates a homomorphism.

This
concludes the proof of the fact that there is a homomorphism from
I_{2}
to

I_{2}^{\dagger}
.

It remains to justify that the common subinstance
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
in
I_{1}
and

I_{2}
is
access-valid in
I_{1}
. Consider one access in
I_{1}
performed with some
method
{\mathsf{mt}}
of a relation
R
, with a binding
{\mathsf{AccBind}}
of values
in
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
, and let us show that we can define a valid output to this
access in
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
.
It is clear by definition of
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
that, if some value of
{\mathsf{AccBind}}
is not
in the domain of
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
, it must be a null introduced in the chase to create

I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
, in the first or in the second step. In this case the only
possible matching facts in
I_{1}
are the facts containing such a null, i.e.,
the facts in
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}\setminus I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
, so these facts are all
in
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}

and there is nothing to show as they can all be returned.

We thus focus on the case when all values of
{\mathsf{AccBind}}
are in
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
. If
{\mathsf{mt}}
is not a
result-bounded access, then we can simply use the fact that
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
is access-valid
in
I_{1}^{\dagger}
to know that all matching tuples in
I_{1}^{\dagger}
were
in
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
, so the
matching tuples in
I_{1}
must be in
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}\cup(I_{1}\setminus I_{1}^{\dagger})
, hence
in
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
. If
{\mathsf{mt}}
is a result-bounded access, then consider the access
on
{\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger}
with the same binding. Either this access returns nothing or it tells
us that there is a fact
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
containing the values of
{\mathsf{AccBind}}
.
In the first case, as
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
is access-valid in
I_{1}^{\dagger}
, we know
that
I_{1}^{\dagger}

contains no matching tuple, hence the constraints of
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
imply
that
I_{1}^{\dagger}
does not contain any
R
-fact which matches
{\mathsf{AccBind}}
in the input
positions of
{\mathsf{mt}}
. This means that any matching tuple in
I_{1}
for the access
on
{\mathsf{mt}}
must be in
I_{1}\setminus I_{1}^{\dagger}
, so they are
in
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
and we can
define a valid output to the access in
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
. This covers the first case.

In the second case, the
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
-fact of
I_{1}^{\dagger}
implies by construction
that
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{*}
, hence
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
, contains infinitely many suitable
facts matching the access. Letting
k
be the result bound
of
{\mathsf{mt}}
, we choose
k
facts among those, and obtain a valid output to the
access with
{\mathsf{AccBind}}
on
{\mathsf{mt}}
in
I_{1}
. Hence, we have shown
that
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
is
access-valid in
I_{1}
.

Hence, we have shown the conditions of Lemma
11
. Using
this lemma, we have completed the proof of Theorem
10
.

FD simplification.

When our constraints include functional dependencies, we can hope for another kind of simplification,
generalizing
the idea of Example
1
:
an FD can force the output of a result-bounded method to be deterministic on a
projection of the output positions.
We will define the
FD simplification
to formalize this intuition.

Given a set of constraints
\Sigma
, a relation
R
that occurs in
\Sigma
, and a
subset
P

of the positions of

R
, we write
{\mathsf{DetBy}}(R,P)
for the set of positions
determined
by
P
, i.e.,
the set of positions
i
of
R
such that
\Sigma
implies the FD

P\rightarrow i
. In particular, we have
P\subseteq{\mathsf{DetBy}}(R,P)
.
For any access method
{\mathsf{mt}}
, letting
R
be the relation that it accesses,
we let
{\mathsf{DetBy}}({\mathsf{mt}})
denote
{\mathsf{DetBy}}(R,P)
where
P
is the set of input
positions of
{\mathsf{mt}}
.
Given a schema
{\mathsf{Sch}}
with result-bounded methods, we can now define its
FD simplification

{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
as follows:

•

The signature of
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
is that of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
plus some new
relations: for each result-bounded
method
{\mathsf{mt}}
, letting
R
be the relation accessed by
{\mathsf{mt}}
,
we add a relation
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
whose arity is

\left|{\mathsf{DetBy}}({\mathsf{mt}})\right|
.

•

The integrity constraints of
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
are those of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
plus,
for each result-bounded method
{\mathsf{mt}}
of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
,
two new
ID
constraints:

\displaystyle R(\vec{x},\vec{y},\vec{z})\rightarrow~

\displaystyle R_{\mathsf{mt}}(\vec{x},\vec{y})

\displaystyle R_{\mathsf{mt}}(\vec{x},\vec{y})\rightarrow~

\displaystyle\exists\vec{z}~R(\vec{x},\vec{y},\vec{z})

where
\vec{x}
denotes the input positions of
{\mathsf{mt}}
and
\vec{y}
denotes the
other positions of
{\mathsf{DetBy}}({\mathsf{mt}})
.

•

The methods of
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
are the methods of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
that have
no result bounds,
plus the following: for each result-bounded method

{\mathsf{mt}}
on relation
R
in
{\mathsf{Sch}}
,
a method
{\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger}
on
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
that has no result bounds and whose input positions
are the positions of
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
corresponding to input positions of
{\mathsf{mt}}
.

Note that the FD simplification is the same as the existence-check
simplification when the integrity constraints
\Sigma
do not imply any FD.
Further observe that, even though the methods of
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
have no result
bounds, any access to a new method
{\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger}
of
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}

is guaranteed to return at most one result. This is thanks to the FD on the
corresponding relation
R
, and thanks to the constraints that relate

R_{\mathsf{mt}}
and
R
.

{exa}

Recall the schema
{\mathsf{Sch}}
of Example
1
and the FD
\phi

on
{\mathsf{Udirectory}}
. In the FD simplification of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
, we
add a relation
{\mathsf{Udirectory}}_{\mathsf{ud}_{2}}(\mathit{id},\mathit{address})
,
we replace
\mathsf{ud}_{2}
by a method
\mathsf{ud}_{2}
on
{\mathsf{Udirectory}}_{\mathsf{ud}_{2}}
whose
input attribute is
\mathit{id}
, and we add the
ID
s

{\mathsf{Udirectory}}(i,a,p)\rightarrow{\mathsf{Udirectory}}_{\mathsf{ud}_{2}}(i,a)
and

{\mathsf{Udirectory}}_{\mathsf{ud}_{2}}(i,a)\rightarrow\exists p~{\mathsf{Udirectory}}(i,a,p)
.
The method
\mathsf{ud}_{2}^{\prime}
has no result bound, but the
ID
s above and the
FD
\phi

ensure that it always returns at most one result.

The point of the FD simplification is that it has no result-bounded methods,
so that, like for the existence-check simplification,
the query containment problem for the schema of the simplification
will not
use any complex cardinality constraints. This is in contrast to the query
containment problem obtained in Example
4.3
.

A schema
{\mathsf{Sch}}
is
FD simplifiable
if every CQ having a monotone plan
over
{\mathsf{Sch}}

has one over the FD simplification of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
. As for existence-check,
if a schema is FD simplifiable,
we can decide monotone answerability by reducing to the same problem in
a schema
without result bounds.

We use a variant of our “blowup process” to show that schemas
with only FD constraints are FD simplifiable:

Theorem 12
.

Let
{\mathsf{Sch}}
be a schema whose constraints are FDs, and let
Q
be a CQ
that is
monotonically answerable in
{\mathsf{Sch}}
. Then
Q
is monotonically answerable
in the FD simplification
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
.

Proof 5.3
.

We will again show the contrapositive of the statement.
Assume that we have a counterexample
I_{1}^{\dagger},I_{2}^{\dagger}
to

{\mathsf{AMonDet}}
for

{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
, with
Q
holding in
I_{1}^{\dagger}
, with
Q

not holding in
I_{2}^{\dagger}
,
and with
I_{1}^{\dagger}
and
I_{2}^{\dagger}
having a common subinstance

I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
that is access-valid
in
I_{1}^{\dagger}
under
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
.
We will upgrade
these to
I_{1},I_{2},I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
having the same property for
{\mathsf{Sch}}
, by
blowing up accesses one after the other. To do so, we initially set

I_{1}
to
be the restriction of
I_{1}^{\dagger}
to the relations of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
, i.e., all
relations but the
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
relations. We define
I_{2}

from
I_{2}^{\dagger}
and

I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
from
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
in the same way.
We fix some valid access selection
\sigma_{1}
for
I_{1}^{\dagger}
that always returns tuples
from
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
when performing accesses with values of
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
.
We consider all possible accesses in parallel,
performing for each access a process described just below.

We consider all the (non-result-bounded) access methods
{\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger}

introduced in
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
— not including the access methods
{\mathsf{mt}}
of

{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
which are simply those without result bounds
in
{\mathsf{Sch}}
. Given such an
{\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger}
, we write
{\mathsf{mt}}
for the corresponding
(result-bounded) access method in
{\mathsf{Sch}}
.
We call
{\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger}
(and also
{\mathsf{mt}}
)
non-dangerous
if the input positions of
{\mathsf{mt}}

determine all positions of the accessed relation. Equivalently,
{\mathsf{DetBy}}({\mathsf{mt}})

contains all positions of
R
. Or again equivalently,
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
and

R_{\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger}
have the same arity. We call
{\mathsf{mt}}
and
{\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger}

dangerous
otherwise.
The blowup process we provide for each access will differ depending on whether
the method is dangerous or non-dangerous.

First, we handle the non-dangerous methods, and simply copy
in
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}

the results of accesses on these methods.
Consider every non-dangerous method
{\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger}
of
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
.
We again write
{\mathsf{mt}}
the corresponding method in
{\mathsf{Sch}}
and use
R
to denote the
relation of the access. We consider every possible access
({\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger},{\mathsf{AccBind}})
on
I_{1}^{\dagger}
with
values in
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
such that one tuple (and, by the FDs, exactly one tuple) is
returned. The
ID
s from
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
to
R
imply there is an
R
-fact with
exactly the same elements in
I_{1}^{\dagger}
and
I_{2}^{\dagger}
. We then add this one fact
to
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
.
We do this for all the non-dangerous methods and accesses using these methods.

Second, we blow up the dangerous methods, which is the complicated step of the
construction.
Consider every dangerous method
{\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger}
of
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
.
Write
{\mathsf{mt}}
for the method in
{\mathsf{Sch}}
corresponding to
{\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger}
and
R
for the relation
of
{\mathsf{mt}}
.
Consider every possible access
({\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger},{\mathsf{AccBind}})
on
I_{1}^{\dagger}
with
values in
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
.
There are two possibilities: either this access returns nothing, or, by the
FDs of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
and the constraints introduced in
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
,
it returns exactly one tuple, which must be in
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
because

I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
is
access-valid in
I_{1}^{\dagger}
.

In the first case, we do nothing. Intuitively, we know that there are no
matching tuples in
I_{1}^{\dagger}
. Now, considering the
ID
constraint in the FD
simplification that goes from
R
to
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
, we infer that there is no

R
-fact in
I_{1}^{\dagger}
whose projection to the input positions of
{\mathsf{mt}}

matches
{\mathsf{AccBind}}
. Thus we will have no problem building a valid answer to this
access.

In the second case, consider the fact
M_{1}^{\prime}
that was returned, following the
access selection
\sigma_{1}

in response to the access
({\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger},{\mathsf{AccBind}})
. Recall that there is an
ID
constraint in
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
that
goes from
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
to
R
. This constraint allows us to infer
from the existence of
M_{1}^{\prime}

that there must be some
witnessing facts
in
I_{1}^{\dagger}

and in
I_{2}^{\dagger}
, namely,

R
-facts whose projection to
{\mathsf{DetBy}}({\mathsf{mt}})
matches
M_{1}^{\prime}
. In this case, we
perform a modification that we refer to as
blowing up the access
. Specifically,
let
X
be the positions of
R
that
are
not
in
{\mathsf{DetBy}}({\mathsf{mt}})
. By our assumption that
{\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger}
is
dangerous,
X
is nonempty.
Construct infinitely many
R
-facts
with all positions in
{\mathsf{DetBy}}({\mathsf{mt}})
agreeing with
M_{1}^{\prime}
,
and with all positions in
X
filled using fresh values
that are different from each other and from other values in
I_{1}^{\dagger}\cup I_{2}^{\dagger}
.
We add these
duplicate facts
to
I_{1}
, to
I_{2}
, and
to
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
.

Performing this process for all accesses in
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
on all dangerous access
methods that return a tuple, we have finished the definition of
I_{1}
,

I_{2}
, and
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
.

Having completed our construction, we now check that the conditions are satisfied.
It is clear that the restriction of
I_{1}^{\dagger}
to the relations of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
is a
subset of
I_{1}
.
We see that

I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}\subseteq I_{1}
and
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}\subseteq I_{2}
, because these two
last inclusions are true initially and all tuples added to
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
are also
added to
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
, or in the case of non-dangerous accesses are already
present in
I_{1}^{\dagger}
and
I_{2}^{\dagger}
. Further,

I_{2}
has a homomorphism back to
I_{2}^{\dagger}
: we can define it as the
identity on
I_{2}^{\dagger}
, and as mapping the fresh elements of every duplicate tuple
of
I_{2}\setminus I_{2}^{\dagger}
to
a witnessing fact in
I_{2}^{\dagger}
. This only collapses fresh values
of
{\mathsf{Adom}}(I_{2})
to values of
{\mathsf{Adom}}(I_{2}^{\dagger})
, and is the identity on constants
of
I_{2}^{\dagger}
.

We must justify that
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
still satisfy the FD constraints
of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
.
This is true because, whenever we add a set of duplicate facts
to
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
, each fact in the set
contains fresh values at the positions of the set
X
, and must match the witnessing
facts already present in
I_{1}^{\dagger}
and
I_{2}^{\dagger}
at the other positions. Hence, if
adding such a duplicate fact violated an FD, the left-hand-side of the FD could not
contain a position in
X
, as elements at these positions are fresh. So the
left-hand-side would be contained in
{\mathsf{DetBy}}({\mathsf{mt}})
. Thus the right-hand-side
would also be contained in
{\mathsf{DetBy}}({\mathsf{mt}})
, because
{\mathsf{DetBy}}({\mathsf{mt}})
is closed
under the FDs. We conclude that if adding the new fact violated an FD, then the
witnessing facts also did, breaking the assumption that
I_{1}^{\dagger}

and
I_{2}^{\dagger}

satisfied the FDs before.

We must now show that
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
is access-valid in
I_{1}
.

To do so, consider a method

{\mathsf{mt}}
of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
and binding
{\mathsf{AccBind}}
.
If
{\mathsf{AccBind}}
contains values
from
{\mathsf{Adom}}(I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}})\setminus{\mathsf{Adom}}(I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger})
, then we know that these values
occur only in tuples from
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}\setminus I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
. Thus
matching tuples in
I_{1}
are all in
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
and there is nothing to show.
Hence, we focus on the case where
{\mathsf{AccBind}}
consists of values
of
{\mathsf{Adom}}(I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger})
.

We first focus on the subcase where
{\mathsf{mt}}
is not result-bounded.
In this subcase,
when performing the same access
({\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger},{\mathsf{AccBind}})
in
I_{1}^{\dagger}
, the valid access
selection
\sigma_{1}
that we fixed
returns all matching tuples in
I_{1}^{\dagger}
, and these tuples
must be part of
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
because
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
is access-valid.
Considering all matching tuples for the access
({\mathsf{mt}},{\mathsf{AccBind}})
in
the larger structure
I_{1}
,
we see they are of
two kinds.
There are those that were already present in
I_{1}^{\dagger}
, which are
in
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}

because as we explained it is access-valid, so
so they are in
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
.
The second kind are those that were added in
I_{1}\setminus I_{1}^{\dagger}
, and they were
added to
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
as well. So in both cases all matching tuples
in
I_{1}

are in
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
.

Now, if
{\mathsf{mt}}
is result-bounded but not dangerous, then performing the access
({\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger},{\mathsf{AccBind}})

on
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
in
I_{1}^{\dagger}
either returned a single
matching tuple or no tuples. If it returned
no matching tuple, then the
ID
from
R
to
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
implies that there was no matching tuple
to the access
({\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger},{\mathsf{AccBind}})
in
I_{1}^{\dagger}
, hence there is still none
in
I_{1}
except potentially those of
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}\setminus I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}^{\dagger}
.
So
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
can be used to construct a valid response. If there is a
matching tuple, then the
ID
from
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
to
R
implies that there is a
matching tuple for the access
({\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger},{\mathsf{AccBind}})
in
I_{1}^{\dagger}
, which we added
to
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
at the end of the construction. So the matching tuple is
in
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
and it is still a valid response to the access
({\mathsf{mt}},{\mathsf{AccBind}})

in
I_{1}
(recall that the FDs imply that this single tuple is the only
possible matching tuple).

We can thus focus on the case where
{\mathsf{mt}}
is result-bounded and dangerous.
In this case, when we considered the access
({\mathsf{mt}},{\mathsf{AccBind}})
in the blow-up
process above for
{\mathsf{mt}}
, either we blew up the access or we did not. If we
did not, then we know that there were no matching tuples in
I_{1}^{\dagger}
for the
access, and the
ID
from
R
to
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
implies that
I_{1}^{\dagger}
contains no
matching tuple for the access
({\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger},{\mathsf{AccBind}})
, so all matching tuples to
the access
({\mathsf{mt}},{\mathsf{AccBind}})
in
I_{1}
are in
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
.
If we did blow the access up, then we know that

I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
contains infinitely many matching tuples to the access that we can use as a
valid response to the access in
I_{1}
. Thus,
I_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}
is indeed
access-valid.

Thus,
Lemma
11
implies that
Q
is not
{\mathsf{AMonDet}}
in
{\mathsf{Sch}}
,
concluding the proof of Theorem
12
.

6.
Decidability of Monotone Answerability

using Existence check and FD simplification

Thus far we have seen a general way to reduce monotone answerability problems with result bounds to query containment
problems (Section
4
). We have also seen schema simplification
results for both FDs and
ID
s, which give us insight
into how result-bounded methods can be used (Section
5
).
We now show that for these two classes of constraints,
the reduction to containment and simplification results combine to
give decidability results, along with tight complexity bounds.

6.1.
Decidability for FDs

We first consider schemas whose constraints consist of FDs.
We start with an analysis of monotone answerability in the case
without result
bounds
:

Proposition 13
.

We can decide whether a CQ is monotonically answerable with respect to a
schema without result bounds whose constraints
are FDs. The problem is
{\mathsf{NP}}
-complete.

Proof 6.1
.

The lower bound already holds
without result bounds or constraints
[
Li03
]
, so it suffices to show the upper
bound.
We know that, by Theorem
4

and Proposition
9
,
the problem reduces to the
{\mathsf{AMonDet}}
query
containment problem
Q\subseteq_{\Gamma}Q^{\prime}
for
{\mathsf{Sch}}
. As
{\mathsf{Sch}}
has
no result bounds, we can define
\Gamma
using the rewriting of the accessibility axioms given
after Proposition
9
.
The constraints
\Gamma

thus consist of FDs and of full TGDs of the form:

\Big(\bigwedge_{i}{\mathsf{accessible}}(x_{i})\Big)\wedge R(\vec{x},\vec{y})\rightarrow R^{\prime}(\vec{x},\vec{y})\wedge\bigwedge_{i}{\mathsf{accessible}}(y_{i}).

Since this is a query containment problem with FDs and TGDs, by Proposition
3

it can be solved by computing the chase.
As the TGDs are full, we know that
we do not create fresh values when computing the chase.
Further, because there are no TGD constraints with primed relations in their
body, once
{\mathsf{accessible}}
does not change, the entire chase process has
terminated.
Besides, when adding values to
{\mathsf{accessible}}
we must reach a fixpoint in
linearly many chase steps
since
{\mathsf{accessible}}
is unary.
Thus the chase with
\Gamma
terminates in linearly many steps. Thus, we can decide
containment by checking in
{\mathsf{NP}}
whether
Q^{\prime}
holds on the chase result,
concluding the proof.

We now return to the situation
with result bounds
. We know that
schemas with FDs are FD simplifiable.
From this we get a reduction to query containment with no result bounds, but introducing
new axioms.
We can show that the additional axioms involving
R_{{\mathsf{mt}}}
and
R
do not harm chase
termination,
so that
{\mathsf{AMonDet}}

is decidable; in fact, it is NP-complete, i.e., no harder than CQ evaluation:

Theorem 14
.

We can decide whether a CQ is monotonically answerable with respect to a schema with
result bounds whose constraints are FDs. The problem is
{\mathsf{NP}}
-complete.

Proof 6.2
.

By Theorem
12
it suffices to deal with the FD simplification, meaning that we can
reduce to a schema of the following form:

•

The signature of
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
is that of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
plus some new
relations: for each result-bounded
method
{\mathsf{mt}}
, letting
R
be the relation accessed by
{\mathsf{mt}}
,
we add a relation
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
whose arity is

\left|{\mathsf{DetBy}}({\mathsf{mt}})\right|
.

•

The integrity constraints of
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
are those of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
plus,
for each result-bounded method
{\mathsf{mt}}
of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
,
two new
ID
constraints:

\displaystyle R(\vec{x},\vec{y},\vec{z})\rightarrow~

\displaystyle R_{\mathsf{mt}}(\vec{x},\vec{y})

\displaystyle R_{\mathsf{mt}}(\vec{x},\vec{y})\rightarrow~

\displaystyle\exists\vec{z}~R(\vec{x},\vec{y},\vec{z})

where
\vec{x}
denotes the input positions of
{\mathsf{mt}}
and
\vec{y}
denotes the
other positions of
{\mathsf{DetBy}}({\mathsf{mt}})
.

•

The methods of
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
are the methods of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
that have
no result bounds,
plus the following: for each result-bounded method

{\mathsf{mt}}
on relation
R
in
{\mathsf{Sch}}
,
a method
{\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger}
on
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
that has no result bounds and whose input positions
are the positions of
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
corresponding to input positions of
{\mathsf{mt}}
.

By Proposition
9
, we then reduce
{\mathsf{AMonDet}}
to query containment.
The resulting query containment problem
involves two copies of the constraints above, on primed and unprimed
copies of the schema, along with accessibility axioms for each
access method (including the new methods
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
).
We can observe a few obvious simplifications of these constraints, when working
with the restricted chase:

•

The “unprimed method-to-regular constraint”,
R_{\mathsf{mt}}(\vec{x},\vec{y})\rightarrow\exists\vec{z}~R(\vec{x},\vec{y},\vec{z})
will never fire, since a fact
R_{\mathsf{mt}}(\vec{a},\vec{b})

is always generated by a corresponding fact
R(\vec{a},\vec{b},\vec{c})
.

•

When firing a constraint of the form
\tau:R^{\prime}(\vec{x},\vec{y},\vec{z})\rightarrow R^{\prime}_{\mathsf{mt}}(\vec{x},\vec{y})
to create a fact
F_{2}=R^{\prime}_{\mathsf{mt}}(\vec{a},\vec{b})
from a fact
F_{1}=R^{\prime}(\vec{a},\vec{b},\vec{c})
,
the fact
F_{2}
will not be a trigger for any rule firing. Indeed, the only rule
applicable to a
R^{\prime}_{\mathsf{mt}}
-fact is the reverse constraint
R^{\prime}_{\mathsf{mt}}(\vec{x},\vec{y})\rightarrow\exists\vec{z}~R^{\prime}(\vec{x},\vec{y},\vec{z})
, for which
F_{1}

witnesses that
F_{2}
is not an active trigger. What is more, the

R^{\prime}_{\mathsf{mt}}
-facts created by constraints of the form
\tau
cannot help make
the query true, as the query does not mention relations of the form
R^{\prime}_{\mathsf{mt}}
. For
this reason, we can disregard unprimed method-to-regular constraints
without changing the query containment problem.

So the constraints that remain in addition to the FDs are:

•

The
ID
constraints
R(\vec{x},\vec{y},\vec{z})\rightarrow R_{\mathsf{mt}}(\vec{x},\vec{y})
where
\vec{x}
denotes the input positions of
{\mathsf{mt}}

and
\vec{y}
denotes the other positions of
{\mathsf{DetBy}}({\mathsf{mt}})
;

•

For every access method
{\mathsf{mt}}
on a relation
S
,
the accessibility axioms which are of the form
\left(\bigwedge_{i}{\mathsf{accessible}}(x_{i})\right)\land S(\vec{x})\rightarrow S_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}(\vec{x})
and
S_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle Accessed}}(\vec{w})\rightarrow S(\vec{w})\land S^{\prime}(\vec{w})\land\bigwedge_{i}{\mathsf{accessible}}(w_{i})
. Note that
S
may be one of the original relations, or
one of the relations
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
, depending on whether
{\mathsf{mt}}
originally had
result bounds or not.

•

The
ID
constraints
R^{\prime}_{\mathsf{mt}}(\vec{x},\vec{y})\rightarrow\exists z~R^{\prime}(\vec{x},\vec{y},\vec{z})
, where
\vec{x}
denotes the input positions of
{\mathsf{mt}}

and
\vec{y}
denotes the other positions of
{\mathsf{DetBy}}({\mathsf{mt}})
.

The only non-full TGDs in these constraints are those of the last bullet
point:
these are the only rules that create new values, and these values
will never propagate back to the unprimed relations. Further,
whenever a primed fact
F
is created containing a null using such a rule,
the only further chase steps that can apply to
F
are FDs, and these will only
merge elements in
F
.
Thus the chase will terminate in polynomially many steps
as in the proof of Proposition
13
,
which establishes the NP upper bound and concludes the proof of
Theorem
14
.

6.2.
Decidability for
ID
s

Next we consider schemas whose constraints consist of
ID
s.
As we already mentioned, Theorem
10
implies
decidability for such schemas. We now give the precise complexity bound:

Theorem 15
.

We can decide whether
a CQ is monotonically answerable
with respect to a schema with result bounds whose constraints are

ID
s. Further, the problem is
{\mathsf{EXPTIME}}
-complete.

Proof 6.3
.

Hardness already holds without result bounds
[
BBB13
]
, so we focus on
the upper bound.
By Theorem
10
, we can equivalently replace
the schema
{\mathsf{Sch}}
with
its existence-check simplification
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
, and
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
does not
have result bounds. Further, it is easy to see that
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
consists only of

ID
s, namely, those of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
plus the
ID
s added in the simplification.
Note that the resulting query containment problem only involves guarded TGDs, and thus we can conclude
that the problem is in
{\mathsf{2EXPTIME}}
from
[
CGLP11
]
. However, we can do better:

[
BBB13
]
showed that the monotone answerability problem for schemas where
the constraints are
ID
s is in
{\mathsf{EXPTIME}}
, and thus we conclude the proof.

6.3.
Complexity for Bounded-Width
ID
s and Special Properties of the Query Containment for Access Methods

Up until now we have seen a reduction of answerability to query answering.
We can see that the query answering problem involves adding auxiliary
constraints —
the “transfer” axioms that capture properties of an access — and
these are of a very special form. Our goal now is to illustrate how the restricted shape
of these axioms can be used to get lower complexity bounds, compared
to what we can get by appealing to coarser classes like guarded or frontier-guarded TGDs.

We illustrate this in an important case for
ID
s, those whose
width
— the number of
exported variables, i.e., of variables shared between the body and the head — is bounded by a constant. Recall that this
includes in particular
UID
s, which have width
1
. For bounded-width
ID
s, it was shown by Johnson and Klug
[
JK84
]

that query containment under constraints is
{\mathsf{NP}}
-complete. This result showed
that the width parameter plays an important role in lowering the complexity of the containment problem.
A natural question is whether the same holds for monotone
answerability.
We accordingly conclude the section by showing
the following, which is new even in the setting without result bounds:

Theorem 16
.

It is
{\mathsf{NP}}
-complete to decide whether a CQ is monotonically answerable with respect to a schema
with result bounds whose constraints are bounded-width
ID
s.

To show this result, we will again use the fact that
ID
s are
existence-check simplifiable (Theorem
10
).
Using Proposition
9
we
reduce to a query containment problem with guarded TGDs. But this is not enough
to get an
{\mathsf{NP}}
bound.
The reason is that the query containment problem includes accessibility axioms, which
are not
ID
s.
So we cannot hope to conclude directly
using
[
JK84
]
.

The rest of this section will be devoted to the proof
of Theorem
16
. As mentioned
above, this will require a finer-grained analysis
of the query containment problem produced from our reduction.
In fact, we will note a particular property of these containment problems that can
be exploited:
they
involve constraints that are
ID
s and
{\mathsf{GTGD}}
s that are “close to
ID
s”:
involving only guards and a fixed set of relations
, specifically, the
{\mathsf{accessible}}

relation. Our results give evidence
that looking at other parameters in query answering problems for tame classes
of dependencies can yield new insights, despite the wealth of results already
present in this area
[
CGL12
,
GMP14
]
.

We begin with the case
without result bounds
, and then extend
to support result bounds.

Proving Theorem
16
without result bounds.

In the absence of result bounds, recall that the
{\mathsf{AMonDet}}
query containment problem

Q\subseteq_{\Gamma}Q^{\prime}
can be expressed as follows:

\Gamma
contains the bounded-width
ID
s
\Sigma
of the schema, their
primed copy
\Sigma^{\prime}
, and for
each access method
{\mathsf{mt}}
accessing relation
R
with input positions
\vec{x}

there is an accessibility axiom:

\Big(\bigwedge_{i}{\mathsf{accessible}}(x_{i})\Big)\wedge R(\vec{x},\vec{y})\rightarrow R^{\prime}(\vec{x},\vec{y})\wedge\bigwedge_{i}{\mathsf{accessible}}(y_{i}).

For each method
{\mathsf{mt}}
, we can rewrite the accessibility axiom above by splitting its head, and
obtain the following pair
of axioms, where the truncated accessibility axioms only create the

{\mathsf{accessible}}

facts (hence the name), and the transfer axioms create the primed facts:

•

(Truncated Accessibility):
\left(\bigwedge_{i}{\mathsf{accessible}}(x_{i})\right)\wedge R(\vec{x},\vec{y})\rightarrow\bigwedge_{i}{\mathsf{accessible}}(y_{i})
.

•

(Transfer):
\left(\bigwedge_{i}{\mathsf{accessible}}(x_{i})\right)\wedge R(\vec{x},\vec{y})\rightarrow R^{\prime}(\vec{x},\vec{y})
.

We let
\Delta
be the set of the truncated accessibility axioms and
transfer axioms that we obtain for all the methods
{\mathsf{mt}}
.

The constraints of
\Delta
are TGDs but not IDs. However, we will take
advantage of their structure to
linearize

\Delta
together with

\Sigma
, i.e., construct a set
\Sigma^{\mathsf{Lin}}

of
ID
s
that “simulate”
the chase by
\Sigma
and
\Delta
.
To define
\Sigma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
formally, we will change the signature.
Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be the signature of the relations used in
\Sigma
, not including
the special unary relation
{\mathsf{accessible}}
used in
\Delta
; and let
w\in\mathbb{N}
be
the constant bound on the width of the
ID
s in
\Sigma
.
We expand
{\mathcal{S}}
to the signature
{\mathcal{S}}^{{\mathsf{Lin}}}
as follows.
For each relation
R
of arity
n
in
{\mathcal{S}}
,
we consider each subset
P
of the positions
of
R
of size at most
w
.
For each such subset
P
,
we add a relation

R_{P}
of arity
n
to
{\mathcal{S}}^{{\mathsf{Lin}}}
.
Intuitively, an
R_{P}
-fact denotes an
R
-fact where the elements
in the positions of
P
are
accessible.

Remember that our goal is to
linearize

\Sigma
and
\Delta
to a set of

ID
s
\Sigma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
which emulates the chase by
\Sigma
and
\Delta
.
If we could ensure that
\Sigma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
has bounded width, we could then conclude
using the result of
[
JK84
]
. We will not be able to enforce this, but
\Sigma^{\mathsf{Lin}}

will instead satisfy a notion of
bounded semi-width
that we now define.

The
basic position graph
of a set of TGDs

\Sigma

is the directed graph whose nodes are the positions of relations
in
\Sigma

with an edge
from position
i
of a relation
T
to position
j
of a relation
U

if and only
if the following is true:
there is a dependency
\delta\in\Sigma
whose body contains an atom
A
using
relation
T
,
whose head atom
A^{\prime}
uses relation
U
, and with an exported variable
x

that occurs at position
i
of
A
and at position
j
of
A^{\prime}
.

We say that

\Sigma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
has
semi-width
bounded
by
w
if it can be decomposed as
\Sigma^{\mathsf{Lin}}=\Sigma^{\mathsf{Lin}}_{1}\cup\Sigma^{\mathsf{Lin}}_{2}

where
\Sigma_{1}^{\mathsf{Lin}}
has width bounded by
w
and
the basic position graph of
\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{Lin}}
is acyclic.
The bound on the semi-width of
\Sigma^{{\mathsf{Lin}}}
then implies an

{\mathsf{NP}}
bound on query containment, thanks
to the following easy generalization of the result of Johnson and Klug
[
JK84
]
:

Proposition 17
.

For any fixed
w\in\mathbb{N}
,
there is an
{\mathsf{NP}}
algorithm for
containment under
ID
s of
semi-width at most
w
.

This is proven by a slight modification of Johnson and Klug’s argument,
so we defer it to
Appendix
C
.

Having defined semi-width, we can now state our linearization result:

Proposition 18
.

For any fixed
w\in\mathbb{N}
,
given a set
\Sigma
of
ID
s of width
w
and a set

\Delta
of truncated accessibility and transfer axioms, and given
a set of facts
I_{0}
,
we can compute in
{\mathsf{PTIME}}
a set of
ID
s
\Sigma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
of semi-width
w

and a set of facts
I_{0}^{\mathsf{Lin}}
satisfying the following:
for any Boolean CQ

Q^{*}
over the primed signature using constants from
I_{0}

and existentially quantified variables,

Q^{*}
is entailed from
I_{0}
,
\Sigma
,
and
\Delta
iff

Q^{*}
is entailed from
I_{0}^{\mathsf{Lin}}

and
\Sigma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
.

The proof of this proposition is our main technical challenge, and it
is deferred to
Section
6.4
. The special form of the
constraints is crucial in getting an efficient linearization that leads
to linear TGDs of small semi-width.

These two results allow us to decide in NP whether the query containment for
{\mathsf{AMonDet}}

holds. Indeed, first rewrite
I_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\mathsf{CanonDB}}(Q)
, along with
\Sigma
and
\Delta

to obtain
I_{0}^{\mathsf{Lin}}
and
\Sigma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
, using
Proposition
18
.
Then, recalling that
\Sigma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
has semi-width
w
,
let
\Gamma_{{\mathsf{Bounded}}}
consist of the primed copy
\Sigma^{\prime}
of the constraints,
along with the
ID
s
of
\Sigma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
that have width
\leq w
;
and let

\Gamma_{{\mathsf{Acyclic}}}
consist of the rules of
\Sigma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
that do not have
width bounded by
w
. By assumption, these rules have an acyclic position
graph.
It is clear that
\Gamma^{\mathsf{Lin}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Gamma_{{\mathsf{Bounded}}}\cup\Gamma_{{\mathsf{Acyclic}}}
also has
semi-width
w
. Now, the following is clear:

{clm}

Let the instance
I_{0}^{\mathsf{Lin}}
and constraints
\Gamma^{\mathsf{Lin}}

be defined from
I_{0}
and
\Gamma
as above.

Then
Q
is
{\mathsf{AMonDet}}
with respect to
{\mathsf{Sch}}

if and only if the chase of
I_{0}^{\mathsf{Lin}}
by

\Gamma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
satisfies
Q^{\prime}
.

Proof 6.4
.

We know that
{\mathsf{AMonDet}}
is equivalent to the containment
Q\subseteq_{\Gamma}Q^{\prime}
with

\Gamma=\Sigma\cup\Sigma^{\prime}\cup\Delta
. This in turn is equivalent
to the existence of a chase proof of
Q^{\prime}
starting with
I_{0}
using chase steps
from
\Gamma
. Thus what we need to show is how to convert such a chase
proof to a chase proof of
Q^{\prime}
starting from
I_{0}^{\mathsf{Lin}}
using steps
of
\Gamma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
, and vice versa.

We start with the converse direction.
It is easy to see that any chase proof of
Q^{\prime}
formed from
I_{0}^{\mathsf{Lin}}

using
\Gamma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
can be converted to a chase proof from
I_{0}
using
\Gamma
. Indeed,
Proposition
18
ensures that
any Boolean CQ over the primed signature that is derivable in
I_{0}^{\mathsf{Lin}}

using
\Sigma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
can be derived in
I_{0}
using
\Sigma
and
\Delta
. And the Boolean CQs that are derivable using
\Gamma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
are those
that can be derived by first applying chase steps
using
\Sigma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
to produce some set
S
of primed facts,
and then applying chase steps involving constraints of
\Sigma^{\prime}
to the facts of
S
. By converting
S
to a Boolean CQ
we see that we can derive a homomorphic image of
S
using

\Sigma
and
\Delta
. Thus all Boolean CQs that
can be obtained from
I_{0}^{\mathsf{Lin}}
using
\Gamma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
can also be obtained using

\Sigma
,
\Delta
, and
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

For the forward direction
we consider a chase proof of
Q^{\prime}
formed from
I_{0}
using
\Gamma
: that is,
using
\Sigma
,
\Sigma^{\prime}
, and

\Delta
. We will show how to obtain a chase proof of
Q^{\prime}
from
I_{0}^{\mathsf{Lin}}
using steps of
\Gamma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
.
We observe that in our input chase proof
we can assume that we first fire rules
of
\Sigma\cup\Delta
to get
a set
S
of primed facts,
and then fire rules of
\Sigma^{\prime}
to get
I^{\prime}
containing all the facts of the
chase proof.
Now, from Proposition
18
we know that
a homomorphic image
S^{\mathsf{Lin}}
of the facts
of
S
can all be derived from
I_{0}^{\mathsf{Lin}}
using
\Gamma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
. As

\Gamma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
contains
\Sigma^{\prime}
, we can also derive
a homomorphic image of the facts of
I^{\prime}

from
S^{\mathsf{Lin}}
, and thus derive homomorphic
images of the facts in the chase proof of
Q^{\prime}
. This justifies that
Q^{\prime}
is also
entailed by
I_{0}^{\mathsf{Lin}}
and
\Gamma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
, concluding the proof.

Claim
6.3
implies that
to solve the
{\mathsf{AMonDet}}
problem, it suffices
determine whether the set of primed facts corresponding to
Q^{\prime}
can be
derived from
I_{0}^{\mathsf{Lin}}
by applying chase steps with
\Gamma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
. This in turn can be
determined using Proposition
17
. This concludes the
proof of Theorem
16
in the case without result bounds.

Proving Theorem
16
with result bounds.

We now conclude the proof of Theorem
16
by handling the case
with result bounds. This will require only slight changes to the prior argument.
By Theorem
10
, for any schema
{\mathsf{Sch}}
whose constraints
\Sigma
are
ID
s,
we can reduce the monotone answerability problem to the same problem for the
existence-check simplification
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
with no result bounds, by
replacing each result-bounded method
{\mathsf{mt}}
on a relation
R
with
a non-result-bounded access method
{\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger}
on a new relation
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
, and expanding

\Sigma
to a larger set of constraints
\Sigma^{\dagger}
,
adding new constraints that capture the semantics of the “existence-check views”
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
:

•

(Relation-to-view):
R(\vec{x},\vec{y})\rightarrow R_{\mathsf{mt}}(\vec{x})
;

•

(View-to-relation):
R_{\mathsf{mt}}(\vec{x})\rightarrow\exists\vec{y}~R(\vec{x},\vec{y})
.

Note that these IDs do not have bounded width, hence we cannot simply reduce to
the case without result bounds that we have just proved. We will explain how to
adapt the proof to handle these IDs, namely,
linearizing using Proposition
18
to
ID
s of
bounded semi-width.

Let us consider the query containment problem for the monotone answerability problem of
\Sigma^{\dagger}
.
This problem is of the form

Q\subseteq_{\Gamma}Q^{\prime}
, where

\Gamma
contains
\Sigma^{\dagger}
, its copy
(\Sigma^{\dagger})^{\prime}
, and the accessibility
axioms. These axioms can again be rephrased. For each access method
{\mathsf{mt}}
on a
relation
R
, letting
\vec{x}
denote the input positions of
{\mathsf{mt}}
, we have the
following two axioms:

•

(Truncated Accessibility):
\left(\bigwedge_{i}{\mathsf{accessible}}(x_{i})\right)\wedge R(\vec{x},\vec{y})\rightarrow\bigwedge_{i}{\mathsf{accessible}}(y_{i})
;

•

(Transfer):
\left(\bigwedge_{i}{\mathsf{accessible}}(x_{i})\right)\wedge R(\vec{x},\vec{y})\rightarrow R^{\prime}(\vec{x},\vec{y})
.

In the above two items the relation
R
can be any of the relations
of
\Sigma^{\dagger}
, including relations of the
original signature and relations of the form
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
. For relations in the original schema,

{\mathsf{mt}}
is an access method of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
that did not have a result
bound. For the new relations,
{\mathsf{mt}}
is a method of the form
{\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger}
introduced in the
existence-check simplification
{\mathsf{Sch}}^{\dagger}
for a result-bounded method
of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
, so
{\mathsf{mt}}^{\dagger}
has no output positions: this means that, in this case,
the (Truncated Accessibility) axiom is vacuous and the (Transfer) axiom further
simplifies to:

\text{(Simpler Transfer):~}\Big(\bigwedge_{i}{\mathsf{accessible}}(x_{i})\Big)\wedge R_{\mathsf{mt}}(\vec{x})\rightarrow R^{\prime}_{\mathsf{mt}}(\vec{x}).

We first observe that in
\Gamma
we do not need to include
the view-to-relation constraints of
\Sigma^{\dagger}
:
facts over
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
can only be formed from the corresponding

R
-fact with the relation-to-view constraint, so triggers of the
view-to-relation constraints will never be active in the chase, and
we know that we can decide
Q\subseteq_{\Gamma}Q^{\prime}
by looking at restricted
chase sequences — i.e., where non-active triggers are never fired — hence removing
view-to-relation constraints makes no difference.
Similarly, we do not need to include the relation-to-view constraints
of
(\Sigma^{\dagger})^{\prime}
. These rules could fire to produce a new

R^{\prime}_{\mathsf{mt}}
-fact, but such a fact could only trigger the corresponding
view-to-relation constraint of
(\Sigma^{\dagger})^{\prime}
, resulting in a state of the chase
that has a homomorphism to the one before the firing of the relation-to-view
constraint. Thus such firings can not lead to new matches.
Thus,
\Gamma
consists now of
\Sigma
, of
\Sigma^{\prime}
,
of (Truncated Accessibility) and
(Transfer) axioms for each method
{\mathsf{mt}}
having no result bound in
{\mathsf{Sch}}
,
and for each method
{\mathsf{mt}}
with a result bound in
{\mathsf{Sch}}
we have
a relation-to-view
constraint from
R
to
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
that comes from
\Sigma^{\dagger}
,
a view-to-relation constraint from
R_{\mathsf{mt}}^{\prime}
to
R^{\prime}

that comes from
(\Sigma^{\dagger})^{\prime}
, and a
(Simpler Transfer) axiom.

We next note that we can normalize chase proofs with
\Gamma
so that the relation-to-view constraints
are applied only prior to (Simpler Transfer).
Thus, for each result-bounded method
{\mathsf{mt}}
of
{\mathsf{Sch}}
,
we can merge the relation-to-view rule
from
R
to
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
, the
(Simpler Transfer) axiom from
R_{\mathsf{mt}}
to
R_{\mathsf{mt}}^{\prime}
, and
the view-to-relation rules from
R_{\mathsf{mt}}^{\prime}
to
R^{\prime}
, into an axiom of the
following form, where
\vec{x}
denotes the input positions of
{\mathsf{mt}}
:

\mbox{(Result-bounded Fact Transfer) }\Big(\bigwedge_{i}{\mathsf{accessible}}(x_{i})\wedge R(\vec{x},\vec{y})\Big)\rightarrow\exists\vec{z}~R^{\prime}(\vec{x},\vec{z}).

To summarize, the resulting axioms
consist of:

•

The original constraints
\Sigma
of the schema;

•

Their primed copy
\Sigma^{\prime}
;

•

The (Truncated Accessibility) and (Transfer) axioms for each access method
without result bounds;

•

The (Result-bounded Fact Transfer) axioms for access methods with result
bounds.

In other words, the only difference with the setting without result bounds is
the last bullet point corresponding to (Result-bounded Fact Transfer).

We will now need
an extension of the linearization result,
Proposition
18
, to handle these additional
constraints:

Proposition 19
.

For any fixed
w\in\mathbb{N}
,
given a set
\Sigma
of
ID
s of width
w
and a set

\Delta
of truncated accessibility, transfer, and Result-bounded fact transfer axioms,
and given
a set of facts
I_{0}
,
we can compute in
{\mathsf{PTIME}}
a set of
ID
s
\Sigma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
of semi-width
w

and a set of facts
I_{0}^{\mathsf{Lin}}
satisfying the following:
for any Boolean CQ

Q^{*}
over the primed signature using constants from
I_{0}

and existentially quantified variables,

Q^{*}
is entailed from
I_{0}
,
\Sigma
,
and
\Delta
iff

Q^{*}
is entailed from
I_{0}^{\mathsf{Lin}}

and
\Sigma^{\mathsf{Lin}}
.

The proof of this will be a variation of the argument for Proposition
18
. It will be explained at the end of Section
6.4
.

Thus we follow the same route as before: linearization,
noting that the special form of our constraints results in linear constraints of bounded
semi-width.
This completes the proof of Theorem
16
in the case with
result bounds.

6.4.
Proof of the Linearization Results
(Proposition
18
and
19
)

We now turn to the missing element in the proof of Theorem
16
, which are our
linearization results, Proposition
18
and its
analog for result-bounded methods,
Proposition
19
.

We first give the intuition on how our linearization works. In the tree-like
chase for guarded TGDs, we have steps that create new nodes, and also
propagation
steps
, that replicate facts across tree nodes. An intermediate goal will be to show
that for guarded TGDs we can get a similar chase where we do not need to propagate
across tree nodes. This will be the
shortcut chase
, defined later, where
we only grow the tree and fire full rules at a given node, with no propagation.
Note that the point of the shortcut chase is not to actually perform or
construct it, but to reason about it.
Once we have defined the shortcut chase and shown
it is complete, it will be easy to perform linearization.
The shortcut chase will make use of full GTGDs that we derive from our
original set of GTGDs. The saturation process that creates these GTGDs will
be a first step.

First we will
review the notion of tree-structured chase proof that is well-known for guarded TGDs

[
CGL12
]
, and show that we can further enforce
the
downward-free
property, where facts only propagate back from a child to its
ancestors in the tree.
This is a step towards simplifying propagation
in the chase.
Second we will need to define a more general notion of
truncated accessibility axioms
,
and give a
{\mathsf{PTIME}}
algorithm for generating the ones that are small enough: this
will give us the full GTGDs that we will need.
Finally we present shortcut chase proofs, where these dependencies
are fired in an even more specific order, and show that this definition of the
chase is still complete.
Lastly we use these tools to prove
Proposition
18
.

Tree-like chase proofs and the downward-free property.

As a step towards our linearization result,
we now present a general result about
chase proofs with
single-headed GTGDs
, i.e., GTGDs having a single atom
in the head. This will be applicable in particular to our analysis
of the chase with
ID
s and candidate truncated accessibility axioms.

For any chase sequence
I_{0}\ldots I_{n}
using single-headed GTGDs, we can associate
a
tree-like chase sequence
, i.e., a sequence
T_{0}\ldots T_{n^{\prime}}
of
chase trees
.
A chase tree

T_{i}
in such a sequence consists of a tree structure with a function
{\mathsf{FactsOf}}_{i}
that maps
each node of
T_{i}
to a collection of facts.
Each
T_{i}
is associated to the instance formed by
unioning all the facts in its nodes, i.e., the union of
{\mathsf{FactsOf}}_{i}(v)
across
all nodes
v
.
Further, if
v
is not the root, there is a fact
F
in
{\mathsf{FactsOf}}_{i}(v)
, the

birth fact of
v
, which serves as a guard for the elements in the
facts of
{\mathsf{FactsOf}}_{i}(v)
. This fact
F
for the node
v
will never change throughout the sequence, so
we denote it by
{\mathsf{BirthFact}}(v)
.

In a tree-like chase sequence
T_{0}\ldots T_{k^{\prime}}
, consecutive chase trees will be linked
by two kinds of steps.
First, there will be
chase steps
, which add a fact to the tree, possibly in a
new node.
If
T_{i+1}
is produced from

T_{i}
by a chase step, this will correspond
to a valid chase step for the two corresponding instances.
Second, a step from

T_{i}
to
T_{i+1}
can be a
propagation step
, which does not change the underlying instance, but
just
copies facts from one node to another, i.e., it modifies
{\mathsf{FactsOf}}
while maintaining the other components.
Both steps are described in detail below.

For the case of
chase steps
, when we perform a chase step to transform
T_{i}
to
T_{i+1}
, we will always
require it to be
tree-friendly
, i.e., we require that
the image of the trigger lies in

{\mathsf{FactsOf}}_{i}(v)
for some node
v
.
When a chase step fires a trigger for a GTGD
\tau
to create a
fact
F
, we choose one such node
v
in which the image lies.
If
\tau
is not full, then we extend the sequence to

T_{i+1}
by adding to
T_{i}
a new node
v^{\prime}
as a child of
v
,
setting
{\mathsf{BirthFact}}(v^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}F
, and setting
{\mathsf{FactsOf}}_{i+1}(v^{\prime})
to be
F
along with any facts in
v
that
are guarded by
F
— these facts are
forward propagated
from
v^{\prime}
to
v
.
If
\tau
is full, then we extend the sequence by defining

T_{i+1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}T_{i}
but changing the function
{\mathsf{FactsOf}}_{i+1}
.
We set
{\mathsf{FactsOf}}_{i+1}(v)
to be
{\mathsf{FactsOf}}_{i}(v)\cup\{F\}
to create
the new fact.

For the case of
propagation steps
, such a step can only take place if the
preceding step was a chase step
with a full GTGD
\tau
.
Letting
F=R(\vec{c})
be the newly created fact, we
consider the set
B_{\vec{c}}
of all nodes that contain a guard for
\vec{c}
. Our process ensures that

B_{\vec{c}}
will form a connected subtree of the chase tree
T_{i}
, and it contains
the node
v
in which the chase step was performed.
We choose a subset
B^{\prime}
of
B_{\vec{c}}
, and propagate the new fact to all
these nodes: for every node
v^{\prime}\in B^{\prime}
, we add
F
to
{\mathsf{FactsOf}}_{i+1}(v^{\prime})
.

Note that, unlike forward propagation, propagation
steps allow us to propagate a fact upwards (from descendants to ancestors) as
well as downwards (from descendant to ancestor). Further, it is optional, i.e.,
we can choose not to propagate.

It is clear that every tree-like chase sequence
T_{0}\ldots T_{n^{\prime}}
induces
a chase sequence in the usual sense of instances
I_{0}\ldots I_{n}
: propagation steps in the
T_{i}
do not result in any
change to the instance, thus
n
may be less than
n^{\prime}
.
We say that such a sequence is a
tree-like chase proof
of some entailment
if the resulting sequence
I_{0}\ldots I_{n}
is, and in addition

T_{0}
consists of only a single node
with
{\mathsf{FactsOf}}_{0}(r)=I_{0}
.
Note that any chase proof with single-headed GTGDs
can be made into a tree-like chase proof: we can always choose to propagate facts
everywhere they are guarded, and then the restriction to tree-friendly chase steps
is without loss of generality.

An example is given in Figure
1
,
and explained in more detail in Example
6.4
just below.
Note that as the chase proceeds, we only add nodes to the chase tree and add facts
to existing nodes. In other words, given a tree node
v
associated to some instance
I_{i}
in a chase
proof,
v
will exist at each later stage
I_{j}
, but may have additional facts.

We will be particularly interested in
the case of proofs with
ID
s and candidate derived truncated accessibility
axioms. In this case the full TGDs include the full
ID
s, as well as the
candidate derived truncated accessibility axioms,
which generate new accessibility facts.

{exa}

We use an example from
[
Kap19
]
.
We consider the initial instance
I_{0}=\{R(c,d)\}
and a set of single-headed GTGDs
\Sigma

\displaystyle R(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\exists y\,S(x_{1},y),

\displaystyle R(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\exists y\,T(x_{1},x_{2},y),

\displaystyle T(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\exists y\,U(x_{1},x_{2},y),

\displaystyle U(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})

\displaystyle\rightarrow P(x_{2}),

\displaystyle T(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\land P(x_{2})

\displaystyle\rightarrow M(x_{1}),

\displaystyle S(x_{1},x_{2})\land M(x_{1})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\exists y\,N(x_{1},y),

the sequence

\displaystyle I_{0}

\displaystyle=\{R(c,d)\},I_{1}=I_{0}\cup\{S(c,d_{1})\},I_{2}=I_{1}\cup\{T(c,d,d_{2})\},I_{3}=I_{2}\cup\{U(c,d,d_{3})\},

\displaystyle I_{4}

\displaystyle=I_{3}\cup\{P(d)\},I_{5}=I_{4}\cup\{M(c)\},I_{6}=I_{5}\cup\{N(c,d_{4})\}

is a chase sequence for
I_{0}
and
\Sigma
.
A corresponding tree-like chase sequence
T_{0},\ldots,T_{8}
is depicted in
Figure
1
.

Figure 1.
Tree-like chase for
Example
6.4
.

The representation is the following.
When we have performed a chase step with a non-full GTGD (e.g., from
T_{0}

to
T_{1}
), the new created node is represented in red, and the node containing
the image of the trigger (its parent node) has a blue border. When such a
step performs forward propagation of facts (e.g., from
T_{7}
to
T_{8}
), the
forward propagated fact are written in blue in the parent node, and in red in
the newly created node. When we have performed a chase step with a full GTGD
(e.g., from
T_{3}
to
T_{4}
), the node containing the image of the trigger
again has a blue border, and the new fact (created in the same node) is in
red. When we have performed a propagation step (e.g., from
T_{4}
to
T_{5}
), the fact being propagated is
written in blue in the node from where we propagate it — namely, the node
where it was created in the previous step by a full chase step, and is
written in red in the nodes where it is propagated. Note that, in these
examples, propagation steps always propagate new facts everywhere they are
guarded.

Our linearization result will rely on the fact that chase proofs can be
normalized to ensure that the propagation of facts in propagation steps only
happens in the “upwards” direction, that is, towards the root of the tree.
Note that this does not affect the propagation of facts to child nodes when
firing a chase step with a non-full GTGD.

{defi}

[Downward-free chase sequence]
We say that a tree-like chase sequence
T_{0}\dotsc T_{n}
with single-headed GTGDs is
downward
-free

if propagation steps always propagate a fact
F
to ancestors of the node where
it is created.
A downward-free
chase proof of an entailment is just a downward-free chase sequence that is a chase proof.

{exa}

We continue with Example
6.4
, taken from
[
Kap19
]
.
The chase depicted in Figure
1
is not
downward-free. Indeed, the propagation step from
T_{6}
to
T_{7}
propagates the
fact
M(c)
to the left child of the rood, which is not an ancestor of the
node where it was created.
However, if we do not propagate it to this node, we can no longer perform the
chase step from
T_{7}
to
T_{8}
to create
N(c,d_{4})
.

Instead, we can redo steps in the chase to make it downward-free. We design a
tree-like chase sequence
T_{0}^{\prime},\ldots,T_{10^{\prime}}
, with
T_{i}=T_{i}^{\prime}
for all
0\leq i\leq 6
. We depict
T_{5}^{\prime}=T_{5}
,
T_{6}^{\prime}=T_{6}
, and
T_{7}^{\prime},\ldots,T_{10}^{\prime}
in
Figure
2
.

Figure 2.
Modification of the chase from Figure
1
to obtain a
downward-free chase.

One can think of downward-free chase sequences as a chase version which is more
similar to the chase with linear TGDs, because it is never useful to propagate
facts when chasing with such TGDs — further chase steps cannot use the
propagated fact to fire a rule.
Another advantage of downward-free proofs is that if we follow
the evolution of a subtree of some node
v
within a proof, what we see
happening in that subtree
is a self-contained proof of all facts derived in the subtree, using
only the initial facts of
v
. Indeed, the chase steps performed afterwards
outside of the subtree will never modify the contents of the subtree.

Proposition 20
.

Let
T_{0}\ldots T_{j}
be a downward-free chase proof. Assume that, in moving
to
T_{i}
, we perform a chase step that creates a node
v
.
Let
T^{\prime}_{i}\ldots T^{\prime}_{m}
be the tree-like chase sequence obtained from
T_{i}\ldots T_{j}
by
restricting to the subtree rooted at
v
in
T_{i}\ldots T_{j}
, eliminating
duplicate consecutive chase trees.
Then
T^{\prime}_{i}\ldots T^{\prime}_{m}
is a downward-free chase proof of the facts of
T^{\prime}_{m}
from
the facts
{\mathsf{FactsOf}}_{i}(v)
of
v
in
T_{i}
.

Proof 6.5
.

Note that
T^{\prime}_{i}
consists of a single node containing the facts of
v

in
T_{i}
.
By the definition of the downward-free chase, the triggers in all chase steps within
T_{i}\ldots T_{j}

used only facts that were generated within the subtree of
v
earlier in the same
sequence in a node in the subtree of
v
. Thus, by an immediate
induction on
m-i
, all these steps can also
be triggered in
T^{\prime}_{i}\ldots T^{\prime}_{m}
. So
T^{\prime}_{i}\ldots T^{\prime}_{m}
is indeed a chase
sequence proving the required facts, and it is downward-free because the original sequence was.

This is a variation of
Corollary 3.1.5 of
[
Kap19
]
. Similar statements appear in our earlier
work
[
AB18b
]
.

We now show the key claim that we can always restrict to downward-free chase sequences:

Theorem 21
.

For every tree-like chase sequence using single-headed GTGDs
T_{0}\ldots T_{n}
, there is a downward-free tree-like chase sequence

T_{0}=\overline{T_{0}},\ldots\overline{T_{m}}

such that there is a homomorphism
h
from the instance of
T_{n}
to the
instance of
\overline{T_{m}}

with
h(c)=c
for any values
c
in the domain of the instance of
T_{0}
.

In particular, if we have a
chase proof of a UCQ
Q
from an instance
I_{0}
using GTGDs
\Sigma
, then
we have downward-free proof of
Q
, starting from
T_{0}
consisting of a single node containing
I_{0}
, applying chase
steps via
\Sigma
.
The proof is based on the idea in Figure
2
.
It is inspired by the conference version of this paper,
and by Proposition 3.1.6 in

[
Kap19
]
. It is presented in Appendix
B
. Note that, in
this downward-free chase, we may need to fire triggers that are not active.

We emphasize that the downward-free chase is never used as an algorithm
to get better complexity bounds directly.
We will only use it to justify steps in our linearization process.

Generalized truncated accessibility axioms and saturation.

Having presented the downward-free chase, we return to the proof of our first
linearization result (Proposition
18
). Recall that
this result
applies to constraints
formed of
ID
s
\Sigma
of width
w
and a set
\Delta
of truncated accessibility and transfer
axioms. Recall that a transfer axiom is of the form:

\Big(\bigwedge_{i}{\mathsf{accessible}}(x_{i})\Big)\wedge R(\vec{x},\vec{y})\rightarrow R^{\prime}(\vec{x},\vec{y}).

In the first step towards linearization, we
will perform a construction that enlarges the truncated accessibility axioms to certain
TGDs that have a similar shape,
which we call
candidate
derived truncated accessibility axioms
. By this we mean
any TGD of the following form:

\Big(\bigwedge_{i\in P}{\mathsf{accessible}}(x_{i})\Big)\wedge R(\vec{x})\rightarrow{\mathsf{accessible}}(x_{j})

where
R
is a relation and
P
is a subset of the positions of
R
.
Notice that the axioms of the form (Truncated Accessibility) defined earlier
can indeed be rewritten to be of this form: the only difference from their original
form is that we have rewritten them
further to ensure that the head always contains a single accessibility fact.

Intuitively, such an axiom tells us that, when a subset of the elements of
an
R
-fact are accessible, then another element of the fact becomes accessible
(by performing an access). We will start by considering what we call the

original truncated accessibility axioms
: these are
simply the (Truncated Accessibility) axioms in the set
\Delta
, which are in
the form above, i.e., with a single

{\mathsf{accessible}}
fact in the head.
For these axioms, the set
P
is the set of input
positions of some method
{\mathsf{mt}}
on
R
.
We will also study candidate derived truncated
accessibility axioms that are not necessarily given in
\Delta
, but which
are semantically
entailed
by the original
truncated accessibility axioms in
\Delta
and by the constraints in
\Sigma
.
By entailment, we always mean the semantic notion discussed in Section
3
,
i.e.,
entailments witnessed
by a chase proof starting with facts in the body of the dependency, concluding
with an instance having a suitable homomorphism from the head of the dependency.
The candidate derived truncated accessibility axioms that are entailed are
simply called the
derived truncated accessibility axioms
.

There can be exponentially many derived truncated accessibility axioms, but we will not
need to compute all of them: it will suffice to compute those of small

breadth
. Formally,
the
breadth
of a candidate derived truncated accessibility axiom is the size of
P
.
Note that the number of possible candidate derived truncated accessibility axioms of breadth

b
is at most
r\cdot a^{b+1}
, where
r
is the number of relations
in the signature and
a
is the maximal arity of a relation. We show that we
can efficiently
compute the derived truncated accessibility axioms of a given breadth, by
introducing a
truncated accessibility axiom saturation algorithm
.

The algorithm iteratively builds up a set
O
of triples
(R,\bar{p},j)
with
\bar{p}
a
set of positions of
R
of size at most
w
and
j
a position of
R
. Each such
triple represents the following candidate derived truncated accessibility axiom of breadth
\leq w
:

\Big(\bigwedge_{i\in\bar{p}}{\mathsf{accessible}}(x_{i})\Big)\wedge R(\vec{x})\rightarrow{\mathsf{accessible}}(x_{j}).

The first step of the algorithm is to set
O\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{(R,\bar{p},j)\mid j\in\bar{p}\}
, representing
trivial axioms.
The algorithm then repeats the steps below:

•

(
ID
):
If we have an
ID

\forall\vec{x}~R(\vec{x})\rightarrow\exists\vec{y}~S(\vec{z})
, where

x_{j_{1}},\dots,x_{j_{m^{\prime}}},x_{j}
(with
m^{\prime}+1\leq w
) are
exported variables that appear respectively
in positions
k_{1}\ldots k_{m^{\prime}},k
within the head atom
S(\vec{z})
,
and if we have
(S,\{{k_{1}}\ldots{k_{m^{\prime}}}\},k)\in O
,
then we add the tuple
(R,\{j_{1}\ldots j_{m^{\prime}}\},j)
to
O
.

The intuition for the (
ID
) step is that derived truncated accessibility axioms
that hold on the target relation
S
can be “propagated upwards” to
R
, i.e., if
an
{\mathsf{accessible}}
fact is created using the
S
-fact, then the same creation
can happen using the
R
-fact.

•

(Transitivity): If there exists a relation
R
, a set of positions
